People hear what I do and suddenly they want to know if they should be buying gold or moving their 401k, even when I'm technically off the clock. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, my wife and even my clients say they barely notice anything wrong with my face, but... I don't know. The photographer at the wedding was using this massive camera, and I was telling my brother that different focal lengths can totally distort how you look, so maybe it's the lighting, but I just can't stop focusing on how uneven my jaw looks.\n\nDoctor: How much is this concern about your jaw and face affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: I mean, I still get through my work. Which is crucial right now, honestly, because the market has been incredibly volatile lately. I had a client call me at 7 AM yesterday absolutely panicking about his tech stocks, and people just get so emotional about money, you know? They read one headline and suddenly they want to liquidate their entire portfolio. I had to spend forty-five minutes just talking him off a ledge. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, my daily life. Yeah, I guess I’ve been a lot slower getting out the door for those client meetings lately. I just get caught up checking the mirror. And even during the appointments, I’m definitely less present than I usually am. I catch myself zoning out, wondering if they're staring at my uneven jaw instead of listening to my advice. \n\nI've even skipped a few art openings recently because of it. My wife loves going to those gallery nights downtown—she actually bought this massive abstract piece for our dining room last month. Tons of blues and yellows. It totally doesn't match the rug at all, but she loves it, so what can you do? We usually go every first Friday, but I just stayed home from the last couple. I just really didn’t want to be seen by all those people, or risk being photographed again.\n\nDoctor: Do you always think your jaw looks uneven, or are there times when you don't notice it as much?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it's pretty much always there in the back of my mind. I guess maybe if I'm really deep into a complex financial model, I might forget about it for a minute. Like the other day, I was running these Monte Carlo simulations for a client who wants to retire early—he's only 52, made a killing in real estate, but he's got all these properties tied up in weird LLCs, and honestly, the tax implications are a nightmare. I was telling him, you can't just sell off a commercial building in this interest rate environment without a solid 1031 exchange plan, right? But he's so stubborn. \n\nAnyway, yeah, unless I'm totally buried in spreadsheets like that, I'm usually thinking about my jaw. Especially if I catch my reflection in a window or something. But mostly, it's just... it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of how uneven your jaw looks change throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Change? Um... I don't really think so. It pretty much just looks like the same amount of unevenness whenever I happen to look. \n\nThough, speaking of the time of day, my whole morning schedule has been completely thrown off lately. I usually stop at this artisanal coffee place on 4th Street on my way to the office—they do this incredible pour-over, Ethiopian beans, really bright and fruity notes. The main barista there, Dave, he's actually trying to get a small business loan to open his own roastery, and he was cornering me about SBA loans the other morning while I was waiting for my cup. I told him, Dave, you've got to have your cash flow projections totally locked down before you even walk into a bank. They don't care how good your espresso is if your margins are too thin, you know? It's like my dad always used to tell me, passion doesn't pay the light bill... \n\nBut, um, wait. What was I just saying? Oh, right, my jaw changing. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed it getting better or worse at different hours. It just always looks off to me.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your jaw and face looking uneven ever completely go away, even for a short time, when you're in a particularly good mood or having a good day?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. Even if I'm having a great day, it's still there. Like, my wife and I went to this incredible farm-to-table place for our anniversary last weekend. We managed to get a reservation at that new spot everyone's talking about—the one where the chef used to run a Michelin-starred place in Chicago? Anyway, the food was unbelievable. They had this heirloom tomato salad with burrata that was just... wow. And the wine pairing, they poured this obscure Italian red that I've never even heard of, but it completely changed the flavor of the lamb. The sommelier was telling us about the volcanic soil in the region, and it's fascinating how much that impacts the grapes. I actually started looking into vineyard investments a few years ago because of things like that, but the overhead is just ridiculous. You really need to be prepared to lose money for the first five to ten years, and most people just don't have the stomach for that kind of illiquidity... \n\nBut, um, sorry. You were asking if the concern goes away when I'm in a good mood. Yeah, even at that dinner, which was fantastic, I still caught myself checking my reflection in the restaurant window a few times. It doesn't just disappear because I'm happy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your jaw might actually look normal to other people, and you're just perceiving it as uneven?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That's actually what brings me in. I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I mean, my wife tells me I'm crazy, and even some of my longer-term clients—like this guy, Richard, who I've been managing a portfolio for since he sold his logistics company back in 2015. Great guy, really sharp. We were playing golf the other week, and he was asking me about municipal bonds versus high-yield corporate debt, because he's getting nervous about the Fed's next move. I was telling him, Richard, you've got to diversify, you can't just chase yield in this market. And I don't know how we got on the topic, but I mentioned my jaw, and he just looked at me like I had two heads. Said he barely notices anything. \n\nBut I see it. Especially after we got those family photos back from my niece's wedding last month. The photographer was terrible, by the way. He charged them an arm and a leg, probably had a 60% profit margin on that package, and he didn't even edit the lighting right. I was looking at the proofs, thinking about how I could write off part of a camera purchase if I started doing headshots for my firm's website, because paying a professional is just throwing money away if they're going to do a rush job like that. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, my wife and people like Richard say they don't see it, or they barely notice it. But I look at those photos, and it's just so obvious to me how uneven and sagging it is. I don't know how they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your jaw looks perfectly normal, but you're just misinterpreting how it looks?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting it? Hmm. I really don't know. I mean, interpretation is a tricky thing, right? It's like when my junior analysts try to interpret a quarterly earnings report. \n\nI had this kid, Jason, just started with us, looking at a mid-cap tech stock the other day. He completely misread their cash flow because he didn't factor in their R&D capitalization. I had to sit him down and say, look, the numbers are the numbers, but if you're looking at them through the wrong lens, you're going to get the wrong picture entirely. You really have to look at the raw data. Actually, that's exactly why I just upgraded my whole home office setup with these new curved 4K monitors. If you're staring at market trends all day, you need absolute clarity. The refresh rate on these things is incredible, totally cuts down on eye strain, which is a huge return on investment when you think about your overall productivity over a ten-hour day. I wrote the whole thing off as a business expense, too... \n\nBut, um, sorry, I'm getting off track again. You were asking if I'm just misinterpreting my face. I honestly couldn't tell you. I mean, I look in the mirror and it just looks obviously uneven to me. I don't really know if it's possible that I'm just seeing it wrong, because to me, it's right there. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your jaw might look perfectly fine to everyone else, but you're the only one who notices something wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it's possible that I'm the only one who really sees it. I mean, my wife says she doesn't, and Richard didn't. It just... it feels so glaring to me. But I suppose people are generally pretty unobservant, you know? \n\nLike, I was at the dealership last week getting my car serviced. I drive a German import, and let me tell you, the maintenance costs are a whole different animal compared to domestic. But the engineering is just flawless. Anyway, I was sitting in the waiting area, and this guy comes in complaining about a rattle in his engine. The service manager listens for three seconds and tells him it's a loose heat shield. The guy had been driving around for weeks and never noticed it until it got so loud he couldn't ignore it. People just don't pay attention to the details until it's right in their face. It's the same with financial portfolios—people don't notice the fee drag on their mutual funds until they realize they've lost tens of thousands over a decade. You really have to read the prospectus, but nobody does. They just trust the glossy brochure...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my jaw. Yeah, I don't know. Maybe they really don't see it. But I do. Every time I look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your jaw being uneven is not based on how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Perception not based on how it actually looks... I mean, I guess anything is possible? But it’s hard to wrap my head around that. Perception versus reality is a tough thing to untangle. \n\nLike... take the commercial real estate market right now. People perceive these downtown office buildings as prime, rock-solid assets because they always have been, right? But the reality is, post-COVID, occupancy rates are just dismal in some of these metros. I have a client who owns a nice mid-rise commercial space downtown, and he absolutely refuses to lower his lease rates because his perception of its value is completely stuck in 2019. I keep telling him, you have to look at the actual cash flow, the reality of the foot traffic. You can't just bank on what you feel it's worth when the market is telling you otherwise. We had this whole argument over lunch at this steakhouse last week—great dry-aged ribeye, by the way, but they overcook the asparagus every single time. It's like they just leave it under the heat lamp... \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, my jaw. Right. I just... I don't know if my brain is playing tricks on me or what you mean by that. Because when I look in the mirror, the sagging is just right there. It looks completely real to me, so I don't really know how to tell if my perception is off. I just see what I see.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern specifically about the unevenness of your jaw or is it more about how your face looks overall?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely specific. The whole reason I'm even here is that I just keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look right around my jawline and this one side of my face. It really kicked into high gear after seeing myself in all these recent family photos. We had a big family get-together, and my wife hired this professional photographer. \n\nWhich, by the way, the rates these event photographers are charging now? Unbelievable. It's pure service-sector inflation at work. I was looking at the invoice, and she charged us something like three grand for maybe four hours of work and a digital gallery link. No prints, just a cloud link! I told my wife, we are in the wrong business. I've got clients managing multi-million dollar retirement portfolios who balk at a standard one percent advisory fee, and here this lady is making almost a thousand bucks an hour just clicking a button on a camera. Honestly, if you look at the barrier to entry for freelance photography versus wealth management, it's insane. You don't even need to pass a Series 7 exam to take a picture, but the profit margins are just... \n\nAnyway, sorry, I'm doing it again. Um, but yeah, it's not my whole face. It's just that one side of my jaw that looks so sagging and uneven to me. Even though my wife and my clients swear they barely notice anything at all, it's just glaring to me in those pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there's nothing actually wrong with your jaw, and it looks normal to everyone else, including in pictures?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? I guess it's technically possible. It's just really hard for me to believe when I look at those pictures. \n\nBut then again, pictures can be deceiving, right? Like, the cameras on smartphones these days use all this computational processing before you even see the image. Apple and Samsung are pouring absolute billions into R&D for that. Actually, I was looking at Apple's quarterly earnings report just the other day. Their services revenue is up, but hardware sales are really plateauing in certain markets. Which makes total sense, really—people are holding onto their devices for three, four years now because the upgrade cycle just doesn't offer enough marginal utility to justify a twelve-hundred-dollar price tag. I had a client ask if they should overweight tech in their IRA right now, and I told them, look, you have to consider the regulatory headwinds in the EU before you go all in on mega-cap tech. The antitrust stuff over there is no joke, and it's going to eat into their margins eventually... \n\nUm, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my jaw looking normal. Yeah, I suppose it's possible it looks fine to everyone else. I just... I can't unsee it myself, you know? Even if there's nothing actually wrong, it just doesn't feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see your jaw as uneven even if multiple doctors or experts told you there was nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I honestly think I would still see it. Even if a whole panel of experts stood there and told me it was perfectly symmetrical, I don't know how I could just shut off what my own eyes are showing me. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you have a piece of information, a data point that just doesn't fit the model, but it's staring you right in the face. Speaking of experts getting things wrong, you wouldn't believe the projections some of these top-tier analysts put out last quarter regarding the Fed's rate hikes. I mean, these are guys with PhDs from Wharton and Chicago Booth, and they were completely off on the inflation stickiness. I had to spend three days straight on the phone walking my older clients off the ledge because they were convinced their fixed-income portfolios were going to vaporize. And the bond market? Total bloodbath. It's just wild how consensus estimates can be so universally accepted by the quote-unquote \"experts\" and then just spectacularly fail when the actual CPI numbers drop. It really makes you question the value of expert forecasting when they're just moving in a herd...\n\nBut, sorry, I'm getting sidetracked again. You asked about my jaw. Yeah, like I said earlier, the whole reason I'm even sitting here is because I just can't stop focusing on this sagging, uneven look on that one side of my face. Ever since I saw those family photos, it's just... stuck in my head. My wife tells me I'm crazy, my clients don't seem to notice a thing, but I just keep getting completely preoccupied with it. So yeah, even if a doctor told me it was fine, I think I'd still be stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your jaw being uneven is a result of your own thoughts or worries, rather than an actual physical issue?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess? It's hard to wrap my head around that. Like, the idea that my brain is just... making it up? Or magnifying it? I suppose it's possible it's just my own worries running away with me. \n\nWorrying is practically my job description, honestly. If I'm not worrying about my jaw, I'm worrying about my clients' asset allocation. You know, behavioral finance is actually fascinating when you think about it. The way human psychology drives market inefficiencies? Like loss aversion—people feel the pain of losing a dollar twice as intensely as the joy of gaining a dollar. I see it every day. A client will have a portfolio that's up twelve percent for the year, but they'll call me in a panic because one single mid-cap stock they picked themselves dropped by five percent. They just fixate on the one negative thing and completely ignore the broader positive context. It's totally irrational, but it's human nature. Richard Thaler won a Nobel Prize for this stuff. I actually read his book \"Misbehaving\" on a flight to Cabo last year. Great read, highly recommend it if you're interested in why smart people make dumb money choices...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? The tangents. My wife says I do this whenever I'm uncomfortable. But, um, to answer your question... yeah. I guess it's entirely possible that this whole jaw thing is just my own thoughts playing tricks on me, just like my clients fixating on that one bad stock. It just... it feels so real when I look in the mirror, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending more than an hour a day thinking about your jaw?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more than an hour. It's... it's a lot. Like I mentioned, what really brings me in here is that I just keep getting completely stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and that one side of my face. It really kicked into high gear after seeing myself in all those family photos we took over the holidays. I just can't stop looking at them, zooming in, comparing angles... \n\nSpeaking of angles, the geometry of market trends right now is just fascinating. If you look at the moving averages on the S&P 500, we're seeing this classic head-and-shoulders pattern forming, which usually signals a bearish reversal. I was mapping it out on my terminal yesterday morning—I usually get into the office around 6 AM before the East Coast markets open—and it's incredible how predictable these chart patterns can be if you just know what to look for. Technical analysis gets a bad rap from the fundamental guys, but I'm telling you, the math doesn't lie. Fibonacci retracements, Bollinger bands... it's like reading the tea leaves of global finance. I actually had a guy come in last week wanting to short the entire semiconductor sector based on a YouTube video he watched, and I had to walk him through the actual chart data to show him why that was financial suicide...\n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. But yeah, the jaw thing. Even though my wife and my clients keep telling me they barely notice anything at all, I'm just constantly preoccupied with it. It definitely eats up way more than an hour of my day, just thinking about it and checking it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, all the time. I don't even need to be in front of a mirror to be focusing on it. It's just sort of running in the background of my mind, you know? Like a ticker tape.

It pretty much just looks like the same amount of unevenness whenever I happen to look. \n\nThough, speaking of the time of day, my whole morning schedule has been completely thrown off lately. I usually stop at this artisanal coffee place on 4th Street on my way to the office—they do this incredible pour-over, Ethiopian beans, really bright and fruity notes. The main barista there, Dave, he's actually trying to get a small business loan to open his own roastery, and he was cornering me about SBA loans the other morning while I was waiting for my cup. I told him, Dave, you've got to have your cash flow projections totally locked down before you even walk into a bank. They don't care how good your espresso is if your margins are too thin, you know? It's like my dad always used to tell me, passion doesn't pay the light bill... \n\nBut, um, wait. What was I just saying? Oh, right, my jaw changing. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed it getting better or worse at different hours. It just always looks off to me.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your jaw and face looking uneven ever completely go away, even for a short time, when you're in a particularly good mood or having a good day?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. Even if I'm having a great day, it's still there. Like, my wife and I went to this incredible farm-to-table place for our anniversary last weekend. We managed to get a reservation at that new spot everyone's talking about—the one where the chef used to run a Michelin-starred place in Chicago? Anyway, the food was unbelievable. They had this heirloom tomato salad with burrata that was just... wow. And the wine pairing, they poured this obscure Italian red that I've never even heard of, but it completely changed the flavor of the lamb. The sommelier was telling us about the volcanic soil in the region, and it's fascinating how much that impacts the grapes. I actually started looking into vineyard investments a few years ago because of things like that, but the overhead is just ridiculous. You really need to be prepared to lose money for the first five to ten years, and most people just don't have the stomach for that kind of illiquidity... \n\nBut, um, sorry. You were asking if the concern goes away when I'm in a good mood. Yeah, even at that dinner, which was fantastic, I still caught myself checking my reflection in the restaurant window a few times. It doesn't just disappear because I'm happy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your jaw might actually look normal to other people, and you're just perceiving it as uneven?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That's actually what brings me in. I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I mean, my wife tells me I'm crazy, and even some of my longer-term clients—like this guy, Richard, who I've been managing a portfolio for since he sold his logistics company back in 2015. Great guy, really sharp. We were playing golf the other week, and he was asking me about municipal bonds versus high-yield corporate debt, because he's getting nervous about the Fed's next move. I was telling him, Richard, you've got to diversify, you can't just chase yield in this market. And I don't know how we got on the topic, but I mentioned my jaw, and he just looked at me like I had two heads. Said he barely notices anything. \n\nBut I see it. Especially after we got those family photos back from my niece's wedding last month. The photographer was terrible, by the way. He charged them an arm and a leg, probably had a 60% profit margin on that package, and he didn't even edit the lighting right. I was looking at the proofs, thinking about how I could write off part of a camera purchase if I started doing headshots for my firm's website, because paying a professional is just throwing money away if they're going to do a rush job like that. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, my wife and people like Richard say they don't see it, or they barely notice it. But I look at those photos, and it's just so obvious to me how uneven and sagging it is. I don't know how they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your jaw looks perfectly normal, but you're just misinterpreting how it looks?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting it? Hmm. I really don't know. I mean, interpretation is a tricky thing, right? It's like when my junior analysts try to interpret a quarterly earnings report. \n\nI had this kid, Jason, just started with us, looking at a mid-cap tech stock the other day. He completely misread their cash flow because he didn't factor in their R&D capitalization. I had to sit him down and say, look, the numbers are the numbers, but if you're looking at them through the wrong lens, you're going to get the wrong picture entirely. You really have to look at the raw data. Actually, that's exactly why I just upgraded my whole home office setup with these new curved 4K monitors. If you're staring at market trends all day, you need absolute clarity. The refresh rate on these things is incredible, totally cuts down on eye strain, which is a huge return on investment when you think about your overall productivity over a ten-hour day. I wrote the whole thing off as a business expense, too... \n\nBut, um, sorry, I'm getting off track again. You were asking if I'm just misinterpreting my face. I honestly couldn't tell you. I mean, I look in the mirror and it just looks obviously uneven to me. I don't really know if it's possible that I'm just seeing it wrong, because to me, it's right there. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your jaw might look perfectly fine to everyone else, but you're the only one who notices something wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it's possible that I'm the only one who really sees it. I mean, my wife says she doesn't, and Richard didn't. It just... it feels so glaring to me. But I suppose people are generally pretty unobservant, you know? \n\nLike, I was at the dealership last week getting my car serviced. I drive a German import, and let me tell you, the maintenance costs are a whole different animal compared to domestic. But the engineering is just flawless. Anyway, I was sitting in the waiting area, and this guy comes in complaining about a rattle in his engine. The service manager listens for three seconds and tells him it's a loose heat shield. The guy had been driving around for weeks and never noticed it until it got so loud he couldn't ignore it. People just don't pay attention to the details until it's right in their face. It's the same with financial portfolios—people don't notice the fee drag on their mutual funds until they realize they've lost tens of thousands over a decade. You really have to read the prospectus, but nobody does. They just trust the glossy brochure...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my jaw. Yeah, I don't know. Maybe they really don't see it. But I do. Every time I look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your jaw being uneven is not based on how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Perception not based on how it actually looks... I mean, I guess anything is possible? But it’s hard to wrap my head around that. Perception versus reality is a tough thing to untangle. \n\nLike... take the commercial real estate market right now. People perceive these downtown office buildings as prime, rock-solid assets because they always have been, right? But the reality is, post-COVID, occupancy rates are just dismal in some of these metros. I have a client who owns a nice mid-rise commercial space downtown, and he absolutely refuses to lower his lease rates because his perception of its value is completely stuck in 2019. I keep telling him, you have to look at the actual cash flow, the reality of the foot traffic. You can't just bank on what you feel it's worth when the market is telling you otherwise. We had this whole argument over lunch at this steakhouse last week—great dry-aged ribeye, by the way, but they overcook the asparagus every single time. It's like they just leave it under the heat lamp... \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, my jaw. Right. I just... I don't know if my brain is playing tricks on me or what you mean by that. Because when I look in the mirror, the sagging is just right there. It looks completely real to me, so I don't really know how to tell if my perception is off. I just see what I see.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern specifically about the unevenness of your jaw or is it more about how your face looks overall?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely specific. The whole reason I'm even here is that I just keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look right around my jawline and this one side of my face. It really kicked into high gear after seeing myself in all these recent family photos. We had a big family get-together, and my wife hired this professional photographer. \n\nWhich, by the way, the rates these event photographers are charging now? Unbelievable. It's pure service-sector inflation at work. I was looking at the invoice, and she charged us something like three grand for maybe four hours of work and a digital gallery link. No prints, just a cloud link! I told my wife, we are in the wrong business. I've got clients managing multi-million dollar retirement portfolios who balk at a standard one percent advisory fee, and here this lady is making almost a thousand bucks an hour just clicking a button on a camera. Honestly, if you look at the barrier to entry for freelance photography versus wealth management, it's insane. You don't even need to pass a Series 7 exam to take a picture, but the profit margins are just... \n\nAnyway, sorry, I'm doing it again. Um, but yeah, it's not my whole face. It's just that one side of my jaw that looks so sagging and uneven to me. Even though my wife and my clients swear they barely notice anything at all, it's just glaring to me in those pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there's nothing actually wrong with your jaw, and it looks normal to everyone else, including in pictures?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? I guess it's technically possible. It's just really hard for me to believe when I look at those pictures. \n\nBut then again, pictures can be deceiving, right? Like, the cameras on smartphones these days use all this computational processing before you even see the image. Apple and Samsung are pouring absolute billions into R&D for that. Actually, I was looking at Apple's quarterly earnings report just the other day. Their services revenue is up, but hardware sales are really plateauing in certain markets. Which makes total sense, really—people are holding onto their devices for three, four years now because the upgrade cycle just doesn't offer enough marginal utility to justify a twelve-hundred-dollar price tag. I had a client ask if they should overweight tech in their IRA right now, and I told them, look, you have to consider the regulatory headwinds in the EU before you go all in on mega-cap tech. The antitrust stuff over there is no joke, and it's going to eat into their margins eventually... \n\nUm, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my jaw looking normal. Yeah, I suppose it's possible it looks fine to everyone else. I just... I can't unsee it myself, you know? Even if there's nothing actually wrong, it just doesn't feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see your jaw as uneven even if multiple doctors or experts told you there was nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I honestly think I would still see it. Even if a whole panel of experts stood there and told me it was perfectly symmetrical, I don't know how I could just shut off what my own eyes are showing me. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you have a piece of information, a data point that just doesn't fit the model, but it's staring you right in the face. Speaking of experts getting things wrong, you wouldn't believe the projections some of these top-tier analysts put out last quarter regarding the Fed's rate hikes. I mean, these are guys with PhDs from Wharton and Chicago Booth, and they were completely off on the inflation stickiness. I had to spend three days straight on the phone walking my older clients off the ledge because they were convinced their fixed-income portfolios were going to vaporize. And the bond market? Total bloodbath. It's just wild how consensus estimates can be so universally accepted by the quote-unquote \"experts\" and then just spectacularly fail when the actual CPI numbers drop. It really makes you question the value of expert forecasting when they're just moving in a herd...\n\nBut, sorry, I'm getting sidetracked again. You asked about my jaw. Yeah, like I said earlier, the whole reason I'm even sitting here is because I just can't stop focusing on this sagging, uneven look on that one side of my face. Ever since I saw those family photos, it's just... stuck in my head. My wife tells me I'm crazy, my clients don't seem to notice a thing, but I just keep getting completely preoccupied with it. So yeah, even if a doctor told me it was fine, I think I'd still be stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your jaw being uneven is a result of your own thoughts or worries, rather than an actual physical issue?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess? It's hard to wrap my head around that. Like, the idea that my brain is just... making it up? Or magnifying it? I suppose it's possible it's just my own worries running away with me. \n\nWorrying is practically my job description, honestly. If I'm not worrying about my jaw, I'm worrying about my clients' asset allocation. You know, behavioral finance is actually fascinating when you think about it. The way human psychology drives market inefficiencies? Like loss aversion—people feel the pain of losing a dollar twice as intensely as the joy of gaining a dollar. I see it every day. A client will have a portfolio that's up twelve percent for the year, but they'll call me in a panic because one single mid-cap stock they picked themselves dropped by five percent. They just fixate on the one negative thing and completely ignore the broader positive context. It's totally irrational, but it's human nature. Richard Thaler won a Nobel Prize for this stuff. I actually read his book \"Misbehaving\" on a flight to Cabo last year. Great read, highly recommend it if you're interested in why smart people make dumb money choices...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? The tangents. My wife says I do this whenever I'm uncomfortable. But, um, to answer your question... yeah. I guess it's entirely possible that this whole jaw thing is just my own thoughts playing tricks on me, just like my clients fixating on that one bad stock. It just... it feels so real when I look in the mirror, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending more than an hour a day thinking about your jaw?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more than an hour. It's... it's a lot. Like I mentioned, what really brings me in here is that I just keep getting completely stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and that one side of my face. It really kicked into high gear after seeing myself in all those family photos we took over the holidays. I just can't stop looking at them, zooming in, comparing angles... \n\nSpeaking of angles, the geometry of market trends right now is just fascinating. If you look at the moving averages on the S&P 500, we're seeing this classic head-and-shoulders pattern forming, which usually signals a bearish reversal. I was mapping it out on my terminal yesterday morning—I usually get into the office around 6 AM before the East Coast markets open—and it's incredible how predictable these chart patterns can be if you just know what to look for. Technical analysis gets a bad rap from the fundamental guys, but I'm telling you, the math doesn't lie. Fibonacci retracements, Bollinger bands... it's like reading the tea leaves of global finance. I actually had a guy come in last week wanting to short the entire semiconductor sector based on a YouTube video he watched, and I had to walk him through the actual chart data to show him why that was financial suicide...\n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. But yeah, the jaw thing. Even though my wife and my clients keep telling me they barely notice anything at all, I'm just constantly preoccupied with it. It definitely eats up way more than an hour of my day, just thinking about it and checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your jaw at times when you're not even looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. I don't even need to be in front of a mirror to be focusing on it. It's just sort of running in the background of my mind, you know? Like a ticker tape. \n\nActually, speaking of ticker tapes, it's a lot like how I track the markets when I'm away from my desk. You don't need to be staring at a Bloomberg terminal to know the Dow is taking a beating. It's been inverted for the longest stretch in history, and I've got all these clients asking if a recession is imminent. I was trying to explain to one of the other dads on the sidelines that the lag effect of monetary policy takes twelve to eighteen months to really hit the real economy, so you can't just look at current employment numbers and assume we're in for a soft landing... \n\nBut, right, sorry. The mirror. Yeah, I'll just be sitting in traffic, or at my desk, nowhere near a mirror, and I'll still be running my hand over my jaw or just picturing how lopsided it looks in my head. So I definitely don't need to be looking at it to be thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's definitely caused some friction. I mean, my wife is frustrated because I'm just... I'm slower getting out the door in the mornings. I'm spending so much time checking my face before I leave for client meetings. And I've even skipped a few art openings we were supposed to go to because I just couldn't stomach the idea of being seen, or worse, being photographed. 

Speaking of art, though, the art market is wild right now as an alternative asset class. I was just reading this piece on fractional ownership of blue-chip art—like, you can buy shares of a Banksy or a Basquiat now. It's an interesting way to diversify away from traditional equities, especially with inflation where it is. Though, honestly, the liquidity is terrible. You're locked in for years, and the valuation models are completely subjective. It's not like valuing a company where you can just run a discounted cash flow analysis. Art is only worth what some billionaire is willing to pay for it at Sotheby's on a Tuesday. I had a client ask if he should put ten percent of his net worth into a Masterworks fund, and I had to talk him off a ledge. I told him, look, unless you're buying it to hang on your wall and enjoy it, you're better off in municipal bonds...

But yeah, anyway, the jaw thing. It's not like I'm completely falling apart. I still get through my work, and I'm functioning, mostly. But I know I'm less present during some of my appointments because I'm distracted by how I look. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting things, even if I'm still getting by.

Like the other day, I was running these Monte Carlo simulations for a client who wants to retire early—he's only 52, made a killing in real estate, but he's got all these properties tied up in weird LLCs, and honestly, the tax implications are a nightmare. I was telling him, you can't just sell off a commercial building in this interest rate environment without a solid 1031 exchange plan, right? But he's so stubborn. \n\nAnyway, yeah, unless I'm totally buried in spreadsheets like that, I'm usually thinking about my jaw. Especially if I catch my reflection in a window or something. But mostly, it's just... it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of how uneven your jaw looks change throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Change? Um... I don't really think so. It pretty much just looks like the same amount of unevenness whenever I happen to look. \n\nThough, speaking of the time of day, my whole morning schedule has been completely thrown off lately. I usually stop at this artisanal coffee place on 4th Street on my way to the office—they do this incredible pour-over, Ethiopian beans, really bright and fruity notes. The main barista there, Dave, he's actually trying to get a small business loan to open his own roastery, and he was cornering me about SBA loans the other morning while I was waiting for my cup. I told him, Dave, you've got to have your cash flow projections totally locked down before you even walk into a bank. They don't care how good your espresso is if your margins are too thin, you know? It's like my dad always used to tell me, passion doesn't pay the light bill... \n\nBut, um, wait. What was I just saying? Oh, right, my jaw changing. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed it getting better or worse at different hours. It just always looks off to me.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about your jaw and face looking uneven ever completely go away, even for a short time, when you're in a particularly good mood or having a good day?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. Even if I'm having a great day, it's still there. Like, my wife and I went to this incredible farm-to-table place for our anniversary last weekend. We managed to get a reservation at that new spot everyone's talking about—the one where the chef used to run a Michelin-starred place in Chicago? Anyway, the food was unbelievable. They had this heirloom tomato salad with burrata that was just... wow. And the wine pairing, they poured this obscure Italian red that I've never even heard of, but it completely changed the flavor of the lamb. The sommelier was telling us about the volcanic soil in the region, and it's fascinating how much that impacts the grapes. I actually started looking into vineyard investments a few years ago because of things like that, but the overhead is just ridiculous. You really need to be prepared to lose money for the first five to ten years, and most people just don't have the stomach for that kind of illiquidity... \n\nBut, um, sorry. You were asking if the concern goes away when I'm in a good mood. Yeah, even at that dinner, which was fantastic, I still caught myself checking my reflection in the restaurant window a few times. It doesn't just disappear because I'm happy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your jaw might actually look normal to other people, and you're just perceiving it as uneven?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That's actually what brings me in. I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. I mean, my wife tells me I'm crazy, and even some of my longer-term clients—like this guy, Richard, who I've been managing a portfolio for since he sold his logistics company back in 2015. Great guy, really sharp. We were playing golf the other week, and he was asking me about municipal bonds versus high-yield corporate debt, because he's getting nervous about the Fed's next move. I was telling him, Richard, you've got to diversify, you can't just chase yield in this market. And I don't know how we got on the topic, but I mentioned my jaw, and he just looked at me like I had two heads. Said he barely notices anything. \n\nBut I see it. He charged them an arm and a leg, probably had a 60% profit margin on that package, and he didn't even edit the lighting right. I was looking at the proofs, thinking about how I could write off part of a camera purchase if I started doing headshots for my firm's website, because paying a professional is just throwing money away if they're going to do a rush job like that. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, my wife and people like Richard say they don't see it, or they barely notice it. But I look at those photos, and it's just so obvious to me how uneven and sagging it is. I don't know how they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your jaw looks perfectly normal, but you're just misinterpreting how it looks?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting it? Hmm. I really don't know. I mean, interpretation is a tricky thing, right? It's like when my junior analysts try to interpret a quarterly earnings report. \n\nI had this kid, Jason, just started with us, looking at a mid-cap tech stock the other day. He completely misread their cash flow because he didn't factor in their R&D capitalization. I had to sit him down and say, look, the numbers are the numbers, but if you're looking at them through the wrong lens, you're going to get the wrong picture entirely. You really have to look at the raw data. Actually, that's exactly why I just upgraded my whole home office setup with these new curved 4K monitors. If you're staring at market trends all day, you need absolute clarity. The refresh rate on these things is incredible, totally cuts down on eye strain, which is a huge return on investment when you think about your overall productivity over a ten-hour day. I wrote the whole thing off as a business expense, too... \n\nBut, um, sorry, I'm getting off track again. You were asking if I'm just misinterpreting my face. I honestly couldn't tell you. I mean, I look in the mirror and it just looks obviously uneven to me. I don't really know if it's possible that I'm just seeing it wrong, because to me, it's right there. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your jaw might look perfectly fine to everyone else, but you're the only one who notices something wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it's possible that I'm the only one who really sees it. I mean, my wife says she doesn't, and Richard didn't. It just... it feels so glaring to me. But I suppose people are generally pretty unobservant, you know? \n\nLike, I was at the dealership last week getting my car serviced. I drive a German import, and let me tell you, the maintenance costs are a whole different animal compared to domestic. But the engineering is just flawless. Anyway, I was sitting in the waiting area, and this guy comes in complaining about a rattle in his engine. The service manager listens for three seconds and tells him it's a loose heat shield. The guy had been driving around for weeks and never noticed it until it got so loud he couldn't ignore it. People just don't pay attention to the details until it's right in their face. It's the same with financial portfolios—people don't notice the fee drag on their mutual funds until they realize they've lost tens of thousands over a decade. You really have to read the prospectus, but nobody does. They just trust the glossy brochure...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my jaw. Yeah, I don't know. Maybe they really don't see it. But I do. Every time I look in the mirror, it's all I can focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your jaw being uneven is not based on how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Perception not based on how it actually looks... I mean, I guess anything is possible? But it’s hard to wrap my head around that. Perception versus reality is a tough thing to untangle. \n\nLike... take the commercial real estate market right now. People perceive these downtown office buildings as prime, rock-solid assets because they always have been, right? But the reality is, post-COVID, occupancy rates are just dismal in some of these metros. I keep telling him, you have to look at the actual cash flow, the reality of the foot traffic. You can't just bank on what you feel it's worth when the market is telling you otherwise. No prints, just a cloud link! I told my wife, we are in the wrong business. I've got clients managing multi-million dollar retirement portfolios who balk at a standard one percent advisory fee, and here this lady is making almost a thousand bucks an hour just clicking a button on a camera. Honestly, if you look at the barrier to entry for freelance photography versus wealth management, it's insane. You don't even need to pass a Series 7 exam to take a picture, but the profit margins are just... \n\nAnyway, sorry, I'm doing it again. Um, but yeah, it's not my whole face. It's just that one side of my jaw that looks so sagging and uneven to me. Even though my wife and my clients swear they barely notice anything at all, it's just glaring to me in those pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there's nothing actually wrong with your jaw, and it looks normal to everyone else, including in pictures?\n\nPatient: I mean... maybe? I guess it's technically possible. It's just really hard for me to believe when I look at those pictures. \n\nBut then again, pictures can be deceiving, right? Like, the cameras on smartphones these days use all this computational processing before you even see the image. Apple and Samsung are pouring absolute billions into R&D for that. Actually, I was looking at Apple's quarterly earnings report just the other day. Their services revenue is up, but hardware sales are really plateauing in certain markets. Which makes total sense, really—people are holding onto their devices for three, four years now because the upgrade cycle just doesn't offer enough marginal utility to justify a twelve-hundred-dollar price tag. I had a client ask if they should overweight tech in their IRA right now, and I told them, look, you have to consider the regulatory headwinds in the EU before you go all in on mega-cap tech. The antitrust stuff over there is no joke, and it's going to eat into their margins eventually... \n\nUm, wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my jaw looking normal. Yeah, I suppose it's possible it looks fine to everyone else. I just... I can't unsee it myself, you know? Even if there's nothing actually wrong, it just doesn't feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see your jaw as uneven even if multiple doctors or experts told you there was nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: I honestly think I would still see it. Even if a whole panel of experts stood there and told me it was perfectly symmetrical, I don't know how I could just shut off what my own eyes are showing me. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you have a piece of information, a data point that just doesn't fit the model, but it's staring you right in the face. Speaking of experts getting things wrong, you wouldn't believe the projections some of these top-tier analysts put out last quarter regarding the Fed's rate hikes. I mean, these are guys with PhDs from Wharton and Chicago Booth, and they were completely off on the inflation stickiness. Total bloodbath. It's just wild how consensus estimates can be so universally accepted by the quote-unquote \"experts\" and then just spectacularly fail when the actual CPI numbers drop. So yeah, even if a doctor told me it was fine, I think I'd still be stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your jaw being uneven is a result of your own thoughts or worries, rather than an actual physical issue?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess? It's hard to wrap my head around that. Like, the idea that my brain is just... making it up? Or magnifying it? I suppose it's possible it's just my own worries running away with me. \n\nWorrying is practically my job description, honestly. If I'm not worrying about my jaw, I'm worrying about my clients' asset allocation. You know, behavioral finance is actually fascinating when you think about it. The way human psychology drives market inefficiencies? Like loss aversion—people feel the pain of losing a dollar twice as intensely as the joy of gaining a dollar. I see it every day. A client will have a portfolio that's up twelve percent for the year, but they'll call me in a panic because one single mid-cap stock they picked themselves dropped by five percent. They just fixate on the one negative thing and completely ignore the broader positive context. It's totally irrational, but it's human nature. Richard Thaler won a Nobel Prize for this stuff. I actually read his book \"Misbehaving\" on a flight to Cabo last year. Great read, highly recommend it if you're interested in why smart people make dumb money choices...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? The tangents. My wife says I do this whenever I'm uncomfortable. But, um, to answer your question... yeah. I guess it's entirely possible that this whole jaw thing is just my own thoughts playing tricks on me, just like my clients fixating on that one bad stock. It just... it feels so real when I look in the mirror, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending more than an hour a day thinking about your jaw?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more than an hour. It's... it's a lot. Like I mentioned, what really brings me in here is that I just keep getting completely stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and that one side of my face. It really kicked into high gear after seeing myself in all those family photos we took over the holidays. I just can't stop looking at them, zooming in, comparing angles... \n\nSpeaking of angles, the geometry of market trends right now is just fascinating. If you look at the moving averages on the S&P 500, we're seeing this classic head-and-shoulders pattern forming, which usually signals a bearish reversal. I was mapping it out on my terminal yesterday morning—I usually get into the office around 6 AM before the East Coast markets open—and it's incredible how predictable these chart patterns can be if you just know what to look for. Technical analysis gets a bad rap from the fundamental guys, but I'm telling you, the math doesn't lie. Fibonacci retracements, Bollinger bands... it's like reading the tea leaves of global finance. I actually had a guy come in last week wanting to short the entire semiconductor sector based on a YouTube video he watched, and I had to walk him through the actual chart data to show him why that was financial suicide...\n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. But yeah, the jaw thing. Even though my wife and my clients keep telling me they barely notice anything at all, I'm just constantly preoccupied with it. It definitely eats up way more than an hour of my day, just thinking about it and checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your jaw at times when you're not even looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. I don't even need to be in front of a mirror to be focusing on it. It's just sort of running in the background of my mind, you know? Like a ticker tape. \n\nActually, speaking of ticker tapes, it's a lot like how I track the markets when I'm away from my desk. You don't need to be staring at a Bloomberg terminal to know the Dow is taking a beating. Just yesterday I was at my daughter's soccer game—she plays travel league, under-14s, they're actually going to regionals next month which is a whole logistical nightmare with booking hotels and flights—but anyway, I'm sitting there in a folding chair on the grass, and I can't stop thinking about the yield curve inversion. It's been inverted for the longest stretch in history, and I've got all these clients asking if a recession is imminent. I was trying to explain to one of the other dads on the sidelines that the lag effect of monetary policy takes twelve to eighteen months to really hit the real economy, so you can't just look at current employment numbers and assume we're in for a soft landing... \n\nBut, right, sorry. The mirror. Yeah, I'll just be sitting in traffic, or at my desk, nowhere near a mirror, and I'll still be running my hand over my jaw or just picturing how lopsided it looks in my head. So I definitely don't need to be looking at it to be thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: It's definitely caused some friction. I mean, my wife is frustrated because I'm just... I'm slower getting out the door in the mornings. I'm spending so much time checking my face before I leave for client meetings. I was just reading this piece on fractional ownership of blue-chip art—like, you can buy shares of a Banksy or a Basquiat now. It's an interesting way to diversify away from traditional equities, especially with inflation where it is. Though, honestly, the liquidity is terrible. You're locked in for years, and the valuation models are completely subjective. It's not like valuing a company where you can just run a discounted cash flow analysis. Art is only worth what some billionaire is willing to pay for it at Sotheby's on a Tuesday. I had a client ask if he should put ten percent of his net worth into a Masterworks fund, and I had to talk him off a ledge. I told him, look, unless you're buying it to hang on your wall and enjoy it, you're better off in municipal bonds...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the jaw thing. It's not like I'm completely falling apart. I still get through my work, and I'm functioning, mostly. But I know I'm less present during some of my appointments because I'm distracted by how I look. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting things, even if I'm still getting by.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I was trying to explain to one of the other dads on the sidelines that the lag effect of monetary policy takes twelve to eighteen months to really hit the real economy, so you can't just look at current employment numbers and assume we're in for a soft landing... \n\nBut, right, sorry. The mirror. Yeah, I'll just be sitting in traffic, or at my desk, nowhere near a mirror, and I'll still be running my hand over my jaw or just picturing how lopsided it looks in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. If I'm not thinking about it, then I'm... well, I'm not thinking about it, you know? So I can't really say how it looks in those moments because my mind is elsewhere. 

Usually, if I'm not thinking about it, I'm completely buried in a spreadsheet. I was actually just building out this massive Monte Carlo simulation for a client who wants to retire at 55. He's got a pretty aggressive portfolio, heavily weighted in emerging markets, which, honestly, is a huge risk right now given the geopolitical situation. I mean, you look at the supply chain disruptions and the currency fluctuations in Southeast Asia, and it's just a recipe for volatility. I had to sit him down and explain sequence of returns risk—like, if you retire and the market tanks in your first three years, your portfolio might never recover, even if the average return over thirty years is eight percent. People just don't understand the math behind early retirement. They see these FIRE movement blogs—Financial Independence, Retire Early—and they think they can just live off dividends from a million bucks. But with inflation eroding purchasing power, a million dollars isn't what it used to be. You really need closer to three or four million if you want to maintain a decent lifestyle in a high-cost-of-living area without eating into your principal...

Um, but to your question, yeah. I don't really know if it looks uneven when I'm not focused on it. I guess if I'm not worrying about it, it's just not on my radar at all.

I guess it's technically possible. It's just really hard for me to believe when I look at those pictures. \n\nBut then again, pictures can be deceiving, right? I guess? It's hard to wrap my head around that. Like, the idea that my brain is just... making it up? Or magnifying it? If I'm not worrying about my jaw, I'm worrying about my clients' asset allocation. You know, behavioral finance is actually fascinating when you think about it. A client will have a portfolio that's up twelve percent for the year, but they'll call me in a panic because one single mid-cap stock they picked themselves dropped by five percent. It just... it feels so real when I look in the mirror, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending more than an hour a day thinking about your jaw?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely more than an hour. It's... it's a lot. Like I mentioned, what really brings me in here is that I just keep getting completely stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and that one side of my face. It really kicked into high gear after seeing myself in all those family photos we took over the holidays. I just can't stop looking at them, zooming in, comparing angles... \n\nSpeaking of angles, the geometry of market trends right now is just fascinating. If you look at the moving averages on the S&P 500, we're seeing this classic head-and-shoulders pattern forming, which usually signals a bearish reversal. I was mapping it out on my terminal yesterday morning—I usually get into the office around 6 AM before the East Coast markets open—and it's incredible how predictable these chart patterns can be if you just know what to look for. Technical analysis gets a bad rap from the fundamental guys, but I'm telling you, the math doesn't lie. Fibonacci retracements, Bollinger bands... it's like reading the tea leaves of global finance. I actually had a guy come in last week wanting to short the entire semiconductor sector based on a YouTube video he watched, and I had to walk him through the actual chart data to show him why that was financial suicide...\n\nUm, anyway. Sorry. But yeah, the jaw thing. Even though my wife and my clients keep telling me they barely notice anything at all, I'm just constantly preoccupied with it. It definitely eats up way more than an hour of my day, just thinking about it and checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your jaw at times when you're not even looking in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. I don't even need to be in front of a mirror to be focusing on it. It's just sort of running in the background of my mind, you know? Like a ticker tape. \n\nActually, speaking of ticker tapes, it's a lot like how I track the markets when I'm away from my desk. You don't need to be staring at a Bloomberg terminal to know the Dow is taking a beating. Just yesterday I was at my daughter's soccer game—she plays travel league, under-14s, they're actually going to regionals next month which is a whole logistical nightmare with booking hotels and flights—but anyway, I'm sitting there in a folding chair on the grass, and I can't stop thinking about the yield curve inversion. It's been inverted for the longest stretch in history, and I've got all these clients asking if a recession is imminent. I was trying to explain to one of the other dads on the sidelines that the lag effect of monetary policy takes twelve to eighteen months to really hit the real economy, so you can't just look at current employment numbers and assume we're in for a soft landing... \n\nBut, right, sorry. The mirror. Yeah, I'll just be sitting in traffic, or at my desk, nowhere near a mirror, and I'll still be running my hand over my jaw or just picturing how lopsided it looks in my head. So I definitely don't need to be looking at it to be thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your jaw affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: It's definitely caused some friction. I mean, my wife is frustrated because I'm just... I'm slower getting out the door in the mornings. I'm spending so much time checking my face before I leave for client meetings. And I've even skipped a few art openings we were supposed to go to because I just couldn't stomach the idea of being seen, or worse, being photographed. \n\nSpeaking of art, though, the art market is wild right now as an alternative asset class. I was just reading this piece on fractional ownership of blue-chip art—like, you can buy shares of a Banksy or a Basquiat now. It's an interesting way to diversify away from traditional equities, especially with inflation where it is. Though, honestly, the liquidity is terrible. You're locked in for years, and the valuation models are completely subjective. It's not like valuing a company where you can just run a discounted cash flow analysis. Art is only worth what some billionaire is willing to pay for it at Sotheby's on a Tuesday. I had a client ask if he should put ten percent of his net worth into a Masterworks fund, and I had to talk him off a ledge. I told him, look, unless you're buying it to hang on your wall and enjoy it, you're better off in municipal bonds...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the jaw thing. It's not like I'm completely falling apart. I still get through my work, and I'm functioning, mostly. But I know I'm less present during some of my appointments because I'm distracted by how I look. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting things, even if I'm still getting by.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw looks uneven even when you're not thinking about it or worrying about what others might think?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know how to answer that. If I'm not thinking about it, then I'm... well, I'm not thinking about it, you know? So I can't really say how it looks in those moments because my mind is elsewhere. \n\nUsually, if I'm not thinking about it, I'm completely buried in a spreadsheet. I was actually just building out this massive Monte Carlo simulation for a client who wants to retire at 55. He's got a pretty aggressive portfolio, heavily weighted in emerging markets, which, honestly, is a huge risk right now given the geopolitical situation. I mean, you look at the supply chain disruptions and the currency fluctuations in Southeast Asia, and it's just a recipe for volatility. I had to sit him down and explain sequence of returns risk—like, if you retire and the market tanks in your first three years, your portfolio might never recover, even if the average return over thirty years is eight percent. People just don't understand the math behind early retirement. They see these FIRE movement blogs—Financial Independence, Retire Early—and they think they can just live off dividends from a million bucks. But with inflation eroding purchasing power, a million dollars isn't what it used to be. You really need closer to three or four million if you want to maintain a decent lifestyle in a high-cost-of-living area without eating into your principal...\n\nUm, but to your question, yeah. I don't really know if it looks uneven when I'm not focused on it. I guess if I'm not worrying about it, it's just not on my radar at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. He's got a pretty aggressive portfolio, heavily weighted in emerging markets, which, honestly, is a huge risk right now given the geopolitical situation. I mean, you look at the supply chain disruptions and the currency fluctuations in Southeast Asia, and it's just a recipe for volatility. I had to sit him down and explain sequence of returns risk—like, if you retire and the market tanks in your first three years, your portfolio might never recover, even if the average return over thirty years is eight percent. People just don't understand the math behind early retirement. They see these FIRE movement blogs—Financial Independence, Retire Early—and they think they can just live off dividends from a million bucks. But with inflation eroding purchasing power, a million dollars isn't what it used to be. You really need closer to three or four million if you want to maintain a decent lifestyle in a high-cost-of-living area without eating into your principal...\n\nUm, but to your question, yeah. I don't really know if it looks uneven when I'm not focused on it. I guess if I'm not worrying about it, it's just not on my radar at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw looks uneven even in pictures where others tell you it looks perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I mean, that's actually the whole reason I'm here. What brings me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It really started hitting me after seeing myself in all these family photos from a recent trip. And the thing is, my wife looks at the exact same pictures and tells me it looks perfectly normal. She says she barely notices anything. Even some of my clients, when I've sort of fished for reassurance, they look at me like I'm crazy. \n\nSpeaking of clients, though, one of them just sent me a photo from this charity gala we sponsored, and all I could look at was my jawline. But the gala itself was a huge success. We managed to raise over two hundred thousand for local literacy programs, which is fantastic. Charitable giving is such an underutilized tool in wealth management, honestly. I'm always trying to get my high-net-worth clients to set up donor-advised funds. It's a brilliant way to take an immediate tax deduction while retaining the ability to grant the money out over time. Plus, if you fund it with highly appreciated stock, you avoid the capital gains tax entirely. I had a guy last week who wanted to sell a massive chunk of Apple stock he's held since the early two-thousands just to write a check to his alma mater. I almost had a heart attack. I told him, just transfer the shares directly to the charity! The tax drag on selling first is just throwing money out the window...\n\nBut, right, the pictures. Yeah, even when people tell me I look fine in them, I still see it. I still see the sagging and the unevenness, no matter what they say."
